The problem asks to evaluate the limit of a quadratic function \( f(x) = 2x^2 - 5x + 1 \) as \( x \) approaches 3 along positive direction. The key steps are:

1. Recognize that for polynomial functions, two-sided limits always exist if they do not result in indeterminate forms.
2. Compute the function at points near 3 to observe behavior:
   - At \( x = 3 \), \( f(3) = 4 \).
   - Points slightly less than 3 (left approach) show increasing values towards 4.
   - Points slightly more than 3 (right approach) also increase beyond 4.
3. Note that since it's a quadratic function with vertex at \( x = 1.25 \), the function is increasing for all \( x > 1.25 \).
4. Conclude that both left-hand and right-hand limits as \( x \) approaches 3 are equal to 4.

Thus, the limit of \( f(x) \) as \( x \) approaches 3 along positive direction is:

\[
\boxed{4}

To solve the given limit problem, we analyze the behavior of the highest degree terms in both the numerator and the denominator as \( x \) approaches infinity.

**Step-by-Step Solution:**

1. **Identify the Degrees of Polynomials:**
   - Numerator: Degree 2 (\(3x^2\))
   - Denominator: Degree 2 (\(5x^2\))

2. **Divide Numerator and Denominator by \( x^2 \):**
   \[
   \frac{3x^2 + 2x + 1}{5x^2 + 4x + 2} = \frac{\frac{3x^2}{x^2} + \frac{2x}{x^2} + \frac{1}{x^2}}{\frac{5x^2}{x^2} + \frac{4x}{x^2} + \frac{2}{x^2}} = \frac{3 + \frac{2}{x} + \frac{1}{x^2}}{5 + \frac{4}{x} + \frac{2}{x^2}}
   \]

3. **Evaluate the Limit as \( x \) Approaches Infinity:**
   - As \( x \to \infty \), the terms \( \frac{2}{x} \), \( \frac{1}{x^2} \), \( \frac{4}{x} \), and \( \frac{2}{x^2} \) all approach 0.
   
   Thus, the expression simplifies to:
   \[
   \lim_{{x \to \infty}} \frac{3 + 0 + 0}{5 + 0 + 0} = \frac{3}{5}
   \]

**Final Answer:**

\boxed{\dfrac{3}{5}}

x³ - 1 = (x - 1)(x² + x*1 + 1²) = (x - 1)(x² + x + 1).

Great, so now the numerator factors into (x - 1) times (x² + x + 1). That's promising because the denominator is also (x - 1), which means I can cancel out this common factor. 

So after simplifying by canceling (x - 1) from both numerator and denominator, I'm left with just (x² + x + 1) in the numerator, and the denominator becomes 1. So the expression simplifies to:

(x² + x + 1)/1 = x² + x + 1.

Now that I've simplified the expression, taking the limit as x approaches 1 is straightforward because it's just plugging in x = 1 into this simpler polynomial. 

Calculating that, we get:
(1)² + 1 + 1 = 1 + 1 + 1 = 3.

Okay, so I have this limit problem to solve: the limit as x approaches infinity of e^x divided by x squared. Hmm, that's lim_{x→∞} (e^x / x²). Alright, let me think about how to approach this.

First off, both the numerator and the denominator are functions that go to infinity as x increases without bound. The numerator is an exponential function, e^x, which grows very rapidly, while the denominator is a polynomial, x squared, which also tends to infinity but perhaps at a slower rate compared to the exponential. So intuitively, I might guess that the exponential in the numerator will dominate over the polynomial in the denominator, meaning the limit should be infinity.

But wait, maybe I need to confirm this more rigorously. In calculus, when dealing with limits involving both polynomials and exponentials at infinity, L’Hôpital’s Rule is often a useful tool. L’Hôpital’s Rule applies to indeterminate forms like ∞/∞ or 0/0. Let's check what happens here.

If I plug in x approaching infinity into e^x / x², both the numerator and denominator approach infinity, so it's an ∞/∞ form, which means L’Hôpital’s Rule can be applied. The rule states that if lim f(x)/g(x) is of the form 0/0 or ∞/∞, then this limit is equal to lim f'(x)/g'(x), provided that latter limit exists.

So, applying L’Hôpital’s Rule once:

f(x) = e^x, so f'(x) = e^x.
g(x) = x², so g'(x) = 2x.

Thus, the limit becomes lim_{x→∞} (e^x / 2x). Now, let's evaluate this new limit. Again, as x approaches infinity, e^x goes to infinity and 2x also goes to infinity, so it's still an ∞/∞ form. Therefore, we can apply L’Hôpital’s Rule a second time.

Taking derivatives again:

f'(x) = e^x remains the same since the derivative of e^x is itself.
g'(x) = 2x becomes g''(x) = 2 (since the derivative of 2x is 2).

So now, applying L’Hôpital’s Rule a second time gives us lim_{x→∞} (e^x / 2). As x approaches infinity, e^x grows without bound, so this limit tends to infinity.

Wait, but does that mean the original limit was also infinity? Yes, because after two applications of L’Hôpital’s Rule, we still get an expression tending to infinity. Therefore, the original limit must be infinity as well.

Alternatively, I can think about the growth rates of e^x and x². The exponential function grows much faster than any polynomial as x approaches infinity. So no matter how large the denominator's degree is (even if it were a higher power like x³ or x⁴), eventually e^x will outpace it. But in this case, since we're only dealing with x squared, it's clear that e^x grows much faster.

To summarize my thought process: I recognized the problem as an indeterminate form ∞/∞, applied L’Hôpital’s Rule twice, each time simplifying the expression but still arriving at infinity. Additionally, understanding the relative growth rates of exponential and polynomial functions supports this conclusion that the limit is indeed infinity.
</think>

{"Final Answer": "The limit is infinity."}

To find the limit of √(x² + 2x) minus x as x approaches infinity, I'll start by analyzing each term.

First, consider the square root expression √(x² + 2x). For large values of x, the x² term dominates, so √(x² + 2x) is approximately equal to x when x is very large. Therefore, subtracting x from this approximation gives a value close to zero.

To get a more precise result, I can factor out x² inside the square root:

√(x² + 2x) = |x| * √(1 + (2/x)).

Since we're dealing with positive infinity, |x| is simply x. This simplifies the expression to x*√(1 + 0). The square root of 1 is 1, so the entire expression becomes approximately x as x approaches infinity.

Subtracting x from this gives:

√(x² + 2x) - x ≈ x - x = 0.

However, there's a more accurate way to compute this limit by rationalizing. Multiply and divide by the conjugate expression √(x² + 2x) + x:

lim (x→∞) [ (√(x² + 2x) - x) * (√(x² + 2x) + x) / (√(x² + 2x) + x) ]

The numerator becomes (x² + 2x) - x² = 2x. So the expression simplifies to:

lim (x→∞) [ 2x / (√(x² + 2x) + x) ]

For large x, √(x² + 2x) ≈ x + (1/x). Therefore, the denominator is approximately x + (x) = 2x. So we have:

lim (x→∞) [ 2x / 2x ] = lim (x→∞) 1 = 1

Wait, that contradicts my initial approximation where I got zero. To resolve this discrepancy, I need to consider a better expansion of √(x² + 2x).

Rewriting the square root:

√(x² + 2x) = x * √(1 + (2/x)).

Using the Taylor series expansion for √(1 + ε) where ε is small:

√(1 + ε) ≈ 1 + (ε)/2 - (ε²)/8 + ...

Here, ε = 2/x. So,

√(1 + 2/x) ≈ 1 + (2/(2x)) - (4/(8x²)) + ... = 1 + (1/x) - (1/(2x²)) + ...

Multiplying by x:

√(x² + 2x) ≈ x*(1 + 1/x - 1/(2x²)) = x + 1 - 1/(2x)

Subtracting x gives:

√(x² + 2x) - x ≈ (x + 1 - 1/(2x)) - x = 1 - 1/(2x).

As x approaches infinity, the term 1/(2x) approaches zero. Therefore,

lim (x→∞) [ √(x² + 2x) - x ] = 1

To find the limit of \(\sqrt{x^2 + 2x} - x\) as \(x\) approaches infinity, we can simplify the expression by rationalizing it.

**Step-by-Step Solution:**

1. **Start with the original limit:**
   \[
   \lim_{{x \to \infty}} \left( \sqrt{x^2 + 2x} - x \right)
   \]

2. **Rationalize the expression by multiplying and dividing by the conjugate:**
   \[
   \lim_{{x \to \infty}} \frac{(\sqrt{x^2 + 2x} - x)(\sqrt{x^2 + 2x} + x)}{\sqrt{x^2 + 2x} + x}
   \]

3. **Simplify the numerator:**
   \[
   (\sqrt{x^2 + 2x})^2 - (x)^2 = x^2 + 2x - x^2 = 2x
   \]
   So, the expression becomes:
   \[
   \lim_{{x \to \infty}} \frac{2x}{\sqrt{x^2 + 2x} + x}
   \]

4. **Factor out \(x\) from the square root in the denominator:**
   \[
   \sqrt{x^2 + 2x} = x \sqrt{1 + \frac{2}{x}}
   \]
   So, the expression becomes:
   \[
   \lim_{{x \to \infty}} \frac{2x}{x\left(\sqrt{1 + \frac{2}{x}} + 1\right)} = \lim_{{x \to \infty}} \frac{2}{\sqrt{1 + \frac{2}{x}} + 1}
   \]

5. **Evaluate the limit as \(x\) approaches infinity:**
   As \(x\) becomes very large, \(\frac{2}{x}\) approaches 0.
   \[
   \lim_{{x \to \infty}} \frac{2}{\sqrt{1 + 0} + 1} = \frac{2}{1 + 1} = 1
   \]

**Final Answer:**
\[
\boxed{1}
\]

   \[
   f(x) = \frac{x^2 - 4}{x - 2}
   \]

   Plugging \( x = 2 \) into the function results in an indeterminate form \( \frac{0}{0} \).

   \[

I need to find the limit of tan(x) divided by x as x approaches 0.

First, I recall that both the numerator and denominator approach 0 as x gets closer to 0. This means the expression is in an indeterminate form (0/0), so L'Hospital's Rule can be applied.

Applying L'Hospital's Rule involves taking the derivatives of the numerator and the denominator.

The derivative of tan(x) with respect to x is sec²(x), and the derivative of x with respect to x is 1.

So, the expression becomes limit as x approaches 0 of (sec²(x)) / 1.

Since sec(0) is 1, sec²(0) is also 1.

Therefore, the limit evaluates to 1.
</think>

To find the limit:
\[
\lim_{{x \to 0}} \frac{\tan x}{x}
\]

**Solution:**

As \( x \) approaches 0:
- The numerator \( \tan x \) approaches 0.
- The denominator \( x \) also approaches 0.

This results in an indeterminate form \( \frac{0}{0} \). We can apply **L'Hospital's Rule**, which states that if the limit of a quotient of two functions is an indeterminate form, then it equals the limit of the quotient of their derivatives.

Taking derivatives:
- The derivative of \( \tan x \) with respect to \( x \) is \( \sec^2 x \).
- The derivative of \( x \) with respect to \( x \) is 1.

Applying L'Hospital's Rule:
\[
\lim_{{x \to 0}} \frac{\tan x}{x} = \lim_{{x \to 0}} \frac{d(\tan x)}{dx} / \frac{d(x)}{dx} = \lim_{{x \to 0}} \frac{\sec^2 x}{1}
\]

Since \( \sec(0) = 1 \):
\[
\lim_{{x \to 0}} \frac{\sec^2 x}{1} = \frac{1^2}{1} = 1
\]

**Final Answer:**

To find the limit of \( x^x \) as \( x \) approaches 0 from the positive side (i.e., \( x \to 0^+ \)), we can use the properties of logarithms and limits. Here's a step-by-step solution:

Let’s denote:
\[
y = x^x
\]
Taking the natural logarithm on both sides to simplify differentiation or evaluation at the limit:
\[
\ln(y) = \ln(x^x) = x \cdot \ln(x)
\]

### Step 2: Evaluate the Limit of \( \ln(y) \) as \( x \to 0^+ \)
We need to find:
\[
\lim_{x \to 0^+} x \cdot \ln(x)
\]
As \( x \) approaches 0 from the positive side, \( \ln(x) \) tends to negative infinity (\( -\infty \)), but it is multiplied by a value approaching 0. The product of these two terms can be evaluated as follows:

\[
\lim_{x \to 0^+} x \cdot \ln(x) = 0
\]
This is because the factor \( x \) approaches zero faster than \( \ln(x) \) approaches negative infinity, resulting in an overall limit of 0.

### Step 3: Find the Limit of \( y \)

\lim_{x \to 0^+} \ln(y) = 0

The derivative of tan(x) can be found using known differentiation rules and trigonometric identities. Here's a step-by-step explanation:

\[ \tan x = \frac{\sin x}{\cos x} \]

**Step 2: Apply the Quotient Rule**
The quotient rule states that if \( f(x) = \frac{g(x)}{h(x)} \), then
\[ f'(x) = \frac{g'(x)h(x) - g(x)h'(x)}{[h(x)]^2} \]

Here, let:
- \( g(x) = \sin x \)
- \( h(x) = \cos x \)

**Step 3: Compute the derivatives of sine and cosine**
\[ g'(x) = \cos x \]
\[ h'(x) = -\sin x \]

**Step 4: Plug these into the quotient rule formula**
\[ f'(x) = \frac{\cos x \cdot \cos x - \sin x \cdot (-\sin x)}{(\cos x)^2} \]

Simplify the numerator:
\[ \cos^2 x + \sin^2 x \]
Using the Pythagorean identity, this equals 1.

**Step 5: Simplify the expression**
\[ f'(x) = \frac{1}{\cos^2 x} \]

Which is equal to:
\[ \sec^2 x \]

**Alternative Approach: Using Standard Derivative Rule**
It's known that:
\[ \frac{d}{dx} (\tan x) = \sec^2 x \]
Or, using the identity \( 1 + \tan^2 x = \sec^2 x \), it can also be expressed as:
\[ 1 + \tan^2 x \]

Both forms are correct and equivalent.

**Final Answer**
The derivative of tan(x) is sec squared x or equivalently, 1 plus tan squared x. Therefore, the final answer is:

```json
{"Final Answer": "sec²x"}
```

\[
\frac{d}{dx}[u(x) \cdot v(x)] = u'(x) \cdot v(x) + u(x) \cdot v'(x)
\]

Let's define our functions:
- Let \( u(x) = \sin(x) \)
- Let \( v(x) = \ln(x) \)

\]

Simplify the expression:

\]

Using the chain rule:
- Let \( f(u) = u^{-1} \) and \( g(x) = x^2 + 1 \).
- The derivative of \( f(u) \) with respect to \( u \) is \( -u^{-2} \).
- The derivative of \( g(x) \) with respect to \( x \) is \( 2x \).
- Applying the chain rule: \( F'(x) = - (x^2 + 1)^{-2} \cdot 2x = \frac{-2x}{(x^2 + 1)^2} \).

- The derivative of the numerator (1) is 0.
- The derivative of the denominator (\( x^2 + 1 \)) is \( 2x \).
- Applying the quotient rule: \( F'(x) = \frac{0 \cdot (x^2 + 1) - 1 \cdot 2x}{(x^2 + 1)^2} = \frac{-2x}{(x^2 + 1)^2} \).

Both methods yield the same result.

To find the derivative of \( F(x) = \ln(\sin x) \), we'll use the **chain rule**. Here's a step-by-step solution:

1. **Identify the outer and inner functions:**
   - Let \( u = \sin x \)
   - Then, \( F(u) = \ln(u) \)

2. **Differentiate each function:**
   - \( \frac{dF}{du} = \frac{1}{u} \) (derivative of \( \ln(u) \))
   - \( \frac{du}{dx} = \cos x \) (derivative of \( \sin x \))

3. **Apply the chain rule:**
   \[
   \frac{dF}{dx} = \frac{dF}{du} \cdot \frac{du}{dx} = \frac{1}{u} \cdot \cos x
   \]

4. **Substitute back \( u = \sin x \):**
   \[
   \frac{dF}{dx} = \frac{\cos x}{\sin x} = \cot x
   \]

**Final Answer:**

To find the derivative of the function \( F(x) = x^3 \ln x \), we will use the **product rule**. The product rule states that if you have a function \( h(x) = u(x)v(x) \), its derivative is:

\[
h'(x) = u'(x)v(x) + u(x)v'(x)
\]

Let's apply this to our function where:
- \( u(x) = x^3 \)
- \( v(x) = \ln x \)

u'(x) = \frac{d}{dx} x^3 = 3x^2
\]

\[
v'(x) = \frac{d}{dx} \ln x = \frac{1}{x}
\]

\[

\]

To find the derivative of \( F(x) = \frac{e^x}{1 + e^x} \), I'll start by recognizing that this is a rational function involving an exponential expression. The best approach here is to use the quotient rule, which allows me to differentiate functions expressed as fractions.

First, I identify the numerator and denominator:
- Numerator (\( u \)) = \( e^x \)
- Denominator (\( v \)) = \( 1 + e^x \)

Next, I find the derivatives of these parts with respect to \( x \):
- The derivative of the numerator (\( u' \)) is also \( e^x \) because the derivative of \( e^x \) is itself.
- The derivative of the denominator (\( v' \)) is \( e^x \) as well, since the derivative of \( 1 \) is zero.

Now, applying the quotient rule formula:
\[ F'(x) = \frac{u'v - uv'}{v^2} \]
Substituting the known values into this formula gives:

To find the derivative of \( F(x) = \frac{e^x}{1 + e^x} \), we can use the quotient rule. The quotient rule states that if you have a function \( \frac{u}{v} \), its derivative is given by:

\[
\left( \frac{u}{v} \right)' = \frac{u'v - uv'}{v^2}
\]

Here, let:
- \( u = e^x \) ⇒ \( u' = e^x \)
- \( v = 1 + e^x \) ⇒ \( v' = e^x \)

Applying the quotient rule:

\[
F'(x) = \frac{e^x (1 + e^x) - e^x \cdot e^x}{(1 + e^x)^2}
\]

Simplify the numerator:

\[
= \frac{e^x + e^{2x} - e^{2x}}{(1 + e^x)^2} = \frac{e^x}{(1 + e^x)^2}
\]

So, the derivative \( F'(x) \) is:

\[
F'(x) = \boxed{\dfrac{e^{x}}{\left(1+e^{x}\right)^{2}}}

\[

\[

To find the derivative of \( F(x) = \frac{x}{1 + x^2} \), we will use the **quotient rule**. The quotient rule states that if you have a function \( F(x) = \frac{u(x)}{v(x)} \), its derivative \( F'(x) \) is given by:

\[
F'(x) = \frac{u'(x) \cdot v(x) - u(x) \cdot v'(x)}{[v(x)]^2}
\]

**Step-by-Step Solution:**

1. **Identify \( u(x) \) and \( v(x) \):**
   \[
   u(x) = x \\
   v(x) = 1 + x^2
   \]

2. **Compute the derivatives \( u'(x) \) and \( v'(x) \):**
   \[
   u'(x) = 1 \\
   v'(x) = 2x
   \]

3. **Apply the quotient rule:**
   \[
   F'(x) = \frac{u'(x) \cdot v(x) - u(x) \cdot v'(x)}{[v(x)]^2} = \frac{1 \cdot (1 + x^2) - x \cdot 2x}{(1 + x^2)^2}
   \]

4. **Simplify the expression:**
   \[
   F'(x) = \frac{(1 + x^2) - 2x^2}{(1 + x^2)^2} = \frac{1 - x^2}{(1 + x^2)^2}
   \]

**Final Answer:**

\boxed{\dfrac{1 - x^{2}}{\left(1 + x^{2}\right)^{2}}}

To find the derivative of \(\sec x\) with respect to \(x\), we'll follow these steps:

1. **Express \(\sec x\) in terms of cosine:**
   
   \[
   \sec x = \frac{1}{\cos x}
   \]

2. **Apply the derivative rule for reciprocals:**

   The derivative of \( \frac{1}{u} \) with respect to \(x\) is:

   \[
   \frac{d}{dx}\left(\frac{1}{u}\right) = -\frac{u'}{u^2}
   \]

   Here, \( u = \cos x \).

3. **Find the derivative of \( \cos x \):**

   \[
   \frac{d}{dx}(\cos x) = -\sin x
   \]

4. **Substitute into the derivative formula:**

   \[
   \frac{d}{dx}\left(\sec x\right) = -\frac{-\sin x}{\cos^2 x} = \frac{\sin x}{\cos^2 x}
   \]

5. **Simplify using trigonometric identities:**

   Recall that:
   
   \[
   \tan x = \frac{\sin x}{\cos x}, \quad \sec x = \frac{1}{\cos x}
   \]
   
   Therefore:

   \[
   \frac{\sin x}{\cos^2 x} = \tan x \cdot \sec x
   \]

**Final Answer:**

\boxed{\tan(x) \cdot \sec(x)}

The limit of (sin x)/x as x approaches 0 is a classic problem in calculus.

Solution:
To evaluate lim_{x → 0} (sin x) / x:

1. **Using Taylor Series Expansion**: The Taylor series expansion of sin(x) around 0 is x - x^3/6 + x^5/120 - ... When divided by x, this simplifies to 1 - x^2/6 + x^4/120 - ..., which approaches 1 as x → 0.

2. **Using L’Hospital’s Rule**: Since both the numerator and denominator approach 0 as x approaches 0, we can apply L’Hospital’s Rule:
   lim_{x→0} (sin x) / x = lim_{x→0} (cos x) / 1 = cos(0) = 1.

3. **Graphical Approach**: The function sin(x)/x is continuous everywhere except at x=0, where it has a removable discontinuity. As x approaches 0 from both sides, the value of sin(x)/x approaches 1.

Therefore, the limit is:

```json
{"Final Answer": "1"}
```

To find the limit as \( x \) approaches infinity for the rational function:

\[
\lim_{{x \to \infty}} \frac{3x^2 + 2x + 1}{5x^2 + 4x + 2}
\]

**Step-by-Step Solution:**

1. **Identify the Highest Degree Terms:**
   - Numerator: \( 3x^2 \)
   - Denominator: \( 5x^2 \)

2. **Divide Both Numerator and Denominator by the Highest Power of \( x \):**
   
   \[
   \lim_{{x \to \infty}} \frac{3x^2 + 2x + 1}{5x^2 + 4x + 2} = \lim_{{x \to \infty}} \frac{\frac{3x^2}{x^2} + \frac{2x}{x^2} + \frac{1}{x^2}}{\frac{5x^2}{x^2} + \frac{4x}{x^2} + \frac{2}{x^2}}
   \]

3. **Simplify the Expression:**
   
   \[
   = \lim_{{x \to \infty}} \frac{3 + \frac{2}{x} + \frac{1}{x^2}}{5 + \frac{4}{x} + \frac{2}{x^2}}
   \]

4. **Evaluate the Limit:**
   
   As \( x \) approaches infinity, the terms \( \frac{2}{x} \), \( \frac{1}{x^2} \), \( \frac{4}{x} \), and \( \frac{2}{x^2} \) all approach zero.

   \[
   = \frac{3 + 0 + 0}{5 + 0 + 0} = \frac{3}{5}
   \]

**Final Answer:**

\[
\boxed{\dfrac{3}{5}}
\]

The problem involves finding the limit as \( x \) approaches infinity of \( \sqrt{x^2 + 2x} - x \). 

To solve this, we rationalize by multiplying by the conjugate:

\[
\lim_{x \to \infty} (\sqrt{x^2 + 2x} - x) = \lim_{x \to \infty} \frac{(\sqrt{x^2 + 2x} - x)(\sqrt{x^2 + 2x} + x)}{\sqrt{x^2 + 2x} + x}
\]

The numerator simplifies to \( (x^2 + 2x) - x^2 = 2x \), so the expression becomes:

\[
\lim_{x \to \infty} \frac{2x}{\sqrt{x^2 + 2x} + x}
\]

Factoring out \( x \) from the denominator gives:

\[
\lim_{x \to \infty} \frac{2x}{x(\sqrt{1 + \frac{2}{x}} + 1)} = \lim_{x \to \infty} \frac{2}{\sqrt{1 + \frac{2}{x}} + 1}
\]

As \( x \) approaches infinity, \( \frac{2}{x} \) approaches 0, so the limit becomes:

\[
\frac{2}{1 + 1} = 1
\]

The limit as x approaches 0 of (tan x)/x can be evaluated using known limits and trigonometric identities.

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Understanding the Problem:**
   We need to find lim_{x→0} (tan x) / x.

2. **Using Small-Angle Approximation:**
   For small values of x, tan x can be approximated by its Taylor series expansion:
   \[
   \tan x = x + \frac{x^3}{3} + \cdots
   \]
   Dividing both sides by x gives:
   \[
   \frac{\tan x}{x} = 1 + \frac{x^2}{3} + \cdots
   \]
   As x approaches 0, higher power terms become negligible.

3. **Applying L'Hospital's Rule:**
   Since both the numerator (tan x) and denominator (x) approach 0 as x→0, we can apply L'Hospital's Rule:
   \[
   \lim_{x\to0} \frac{\tan x}{x} = \lim_{x\to0} \frac{d/dx (\tan x)}{d/dx (x)} = \lim_{x\to0} \frac{\sec^2 x}{1}
   \]
   At x=0, sec²(0) = 1.

4. **Verification with Trigonometric Identities:**
   Alternatively, rewrite tan x as sin x / cos x:
   \[
   \frac{\tan x}{x} = \frac{\sin x}{x \cos x}
   \]
   The limit of (sin x)/x is 1, and the limit of cos x approaches 1. Thus,
   \[
   \lim_{x\to0} \frac{\tan x}{x} = 1
   \]

To find the limit:
\[
\lim_{{x \to 0}} \frac{\ln(1 + x)}{x}
\]

**Step-by-Step Solution:**

   - Attempting to directly substitute \( x = 0 \) into the function results in an indeterminate form:
     \[
     \frac{\ln(1 + 0)}{0} = \frac{0}{0}
     \]
   - Since this is an indeterminate form, we can apply **L'Hospital's Rule**, which states that for limits of the form \( \frac{0}{0} \) or \( \frac{\infty}{\infty} \), the limit can be found by taking the derivatives of the numerator and denominator.

2. **Applying L'Hospital's Rule:**
   - Differentiate the numerator and the denominator with respect to \( x \):
     \[
     \text{Numerator derivative: } \frac{d}{dx}[\ln(1 + x)] = \frac{1}{1 + x}
     \]
     \[
     \text{Denominator derivative: } \frac{d}{dx}[x] = 1
     \]
   - Now, evaluate the limit using these derivatives:
     \[

To determine the limit of ln(1 + x)/x as x approaches 0 from the positive side, I recognize that both the numerator and denominator approach zero. This suggests using L'Hospital's Rule since we have an indeterminate form of 0/0.

Applying L'Hospital's Rule involves taking the derivatives of the numerator and the denominator with respect to x. The derivative of ln(1 + x) is 1/(1 + x), and the derivative of x is 1. This simplifies the expression to 1/(1 + x).

As x approaches 0, 1/(1 + x) approaches 1/1, which is simply 1.

Therefore, the limit evaluates to 1.
</think>

To find the limit:
\[
\lim_{{x \to 0^+}} \frac{\ln(1+x)}{x}
\]
we can use L'Hospital's Rule because both the numerator and denominator approach zero as \( x \) approaches 0 from the positive side.

**Step-by-Step Solution:**

1. **Identify the Indeterminate Form:**
   - As \( x \to 0^+ \), \( \ln(1+x) \to \ln(1) = 0 \).
   - The denominator \( x \to 0 \).
   - Thus, we have a \( \frac{0}{0} \) indeterminate form.

2. **Apply L'Hospital's Rule:**
   \[
   \lim_{{x \to 0^+}} \frac{\ln(1+x)}{x} = \lim_{{x \to 0^+}} \frac{\frac{d}{dx}\ln(1+x)}{\frac{d}{dx}x}
   \]
   
3. **Differentiate the Numerator and Denominator:**
   - \( \frac{d}{dx}\ln(1+x) = \frac{1}{1+x} \)
   - \( \frac{d}{dx}x = 1 \)
   
4. **Simplify the Expression:**
   \[
   \lim_{{x \to 0^+}} \frac{\frac{1}{1+x}}{1} = \lim_{{x \to 0^+}} \frac{1}{1+x}
   \]
   
5. **Evaluate the Limit:**
   - As \( x \to 0^+ \), \( 1 + x \to 1 \).
   - Therefore, \( \frac{1}{1+x} \to \frac{1}{1} = 1 \).

**Final Answer:**

\[
\boxed{1}
\]

To find the limit as \( x \) approaches 0 of \( \frac{x - \tan x}{x^3} \), we can use the Taylor series expansion of \( \tan x \).

The Taylor series for \( \tan x \) around 0 is:
\[ \tan x = x + \frac{x^3}{3} + \frac{2x^5}{15} + \cdots \]

Subtracting \( x \) from both sides gives:
\[ \tan x - x = \frac{x^3}{3} + \frac{2x^5}{15} + \cdots \]
So,
\[ x - \tan x = -\left( \frac{x^3}{3} + \frac{2x^5}{15} + \cdots \right) \]

Substituting into the limit expression:
\[ \lim_{x \to 0} \frac{x - \tan x}{x^3} = \lim_{x \to 0} \frac{-\left( \frac{x^3}{3} + \frac{2x^5}{15} + \cdots \right)}{x^3} \]
\[ = \lim_{x \to 0} -\left( \frac{1}{3} + \frac{2x^2}{15} + \cdots \right) \]

As \( x \) approaches 0, the higher-order terms vanish, leaving:
\[ = -\frac{1}{3} \]

**Answer:**  
The limit is \( -\frac{1}{3} \).

To find the limit of \(\frac{\ln x}{x}\) as \(x\) approaches infinity, we can use L'Hospital's Rule since both the numerator and denominator approach infinity, resulting in an indeterminate form \(\frac{\infty}{\infty}\).

**Solution:**

1. **Apply L'Hospital's Rule:**
   
   The derivative of \(\ln x\) with respect to \(x\) is \(\frac{1}{x}\), and the derivative of \(x\) with respect to \(x\) is 1.

   \[
   \lim_{x \to \infty} \frac{\ln x}{x} = \lim_{x \to \infty} \frac{\frac{d}{dx} (\ln x)}{\frac{d}{dx} (x)} = \lim_{x \to \infty} \frac{\frac{1}{x}}{1}
   \]

   \lim_{x \to \infty} \frac{1}{x}
   \]

Therefore, I think my first thought was incorrect because I directly evaluated at x=1 and saw a non-zero numerator over zero denominator, but using derivatives clarifies that it approaches a finite limit as x approaches 1 smoothly, which is indeed correct.
</think>

The limit can be found by recognizing the form of the difference quotient in the definition of the derivative. By applying L'Hôpital's Rule or computing the derivative directly, we find:

Answer: {"Final Answer": "2^{-2/3}"}

To find the derivative of the function \( F(x) = 3x^2 - 5x + 4 \), we'll use basic rules of differentiation.

### Step-by-Step Solution:

1. **Differentiate each term separately:**
   
   - **First term:** \( 3x^2 \)
     \[
     \frac{d}{dx}(3x^2) = 3 \times 2x^{2-1} = 6x
     \]
     
   - **Second term:** \( -5x \)
     \[
     \frac{d}{dx}(-5x) = -5
     \]
     
   - **Third term:** \( 4 \) (constant)
     \[
     \frac{d}{dx}(4) = 0
     \]

2. **Combine the derivatives of each term:**
   
   \[
   F'(x) = 6x - 5 + 0 = 6x - 5
   \]

### Final Answer:

The derivative of \( F(x) = 3x^2 - 5x + 4 \) is:
\[
\boxed{6x - 5}
\]

Alright, I've got this problem to solve: find the derivative of \( \frac{1}{x} \). Okay, derivatives. Let's see... I remember from my calculus class that the derivative measures how a function changes as its input changes. In this case, we're dealing with \( f(x) = \frac{1}{x} \).

First, I think about what \( \frac{1}{x} \) looks like. It's a hyperbola, right? It approaches zero as x goes to infinity and has vertical asymptotes at x=0. But that's more about its graph; the derivative is all about the rate of change.

I recall some basic derivative rules. The power rule comes to mind! Yes, the power rule states that if \( f(x) = x^n \), then \( f'(x) = n \cdot x^{n-1} \). So maybe I can rewrite \( \frac{1}{x} \) in a form that resembles \( x^n \).

Let's see... \( \frac{1}{x} \) is the same as \( x^{-1} \). Ah, yes! That looks like it fits the power rule. So applying the power rule to \( x^{-1} \):

\( f(x) = x^{-1} \)

So, using the power rule:

\( f'(x) = -1 \cdot x^{-1-1} = -x^{-2} \)

Simplifying that, we get:

\( f'(x) = -\frac{1}{x^2} \)

Wait, is there another way to approach this? Maybe using the limit definition of a derivative?

Yes, the limit definition is \( f'(a) = \lim_{h \to 0} \frac{f(a+h) - f(a)}{h} \).

Let's try that:

\( f(x) = \frac{1}{x} \)

So,

\[
f'(x) = \lim_{h \to 0} \frac{\frac{1}{x + h} - \frac{1}{x}}{h}
\]

Simplify the numerator by finding a common denominator:

\[
= \lim_{h \to 0} \frac{\frac{x - (x + h)}{x(x + h)}}{h}
\]
\[
= \lim_{h \to 0} \frac{\frac{-h}{x(x + h)}}{h}
\]

The \( h \) in the numerator and denominator cancels out:

\[
= \lim_{h \to 0} \frac{-1}{x(x + h)}
\]

As \( h \) approaches 0, \( x + h \) becomes just \( x \):

\[
f'(x) = -\frac{1}{x^2}
\]

Okay, so both methods give the same result. That's reassuring.

Is there anything else I should check? Maybe verifying with known derivatives of standard functions or consulting a derivative table to confirm that this aligns with established rules.

Looking it up, yes! The derivative of \( x^n \) is indeed \( n x^{n-1} \), so my application of the power rule was correct. Plus, using both the limit definition and the power rule converged on the same answer, which gives me confidence that I didn't make a mistake.

Also, thinking about the graph of \( f(x) = \frac{1}{x} \), its derivative being negative everywhere except at x=0 (where it's undefined) makes sense because the function is decreasing in both the positive and negative directions. That visual intuition matches the mathematical result.

So, after all this checking, I'm pretty sure that the derivative of \( \frac{1}{x} \) with respect to x is \( -\frac{1}{x^2} \).
</think>

Differentiating term by term:
- The derivative of \( x^4 \) is \( 4x^3 \).
- The derivative of \( -3x^3 \) is \( -9x^2 \).
- The derivative of \( x \) is 1.

Adding these together, the first derivative F'(x) = \( 4x^3 - 9x^2 + 1 \).

Next, I'll find the second derivative by differentiating F'(x):
- The derivative of \( 4x^3 \) is \( 12x^2 \).

To find the **second derivative** of the function:

\[
F(x) = x^4 - 3x^3 + x
\]

we follow these steps:

### Step 1: Find the First Derivative (\( F'(x) \))

Differentiate \( F(x) \) with respect to \( x \):

\[
F'(x) = \frac{d}{dx}(x^4) - \frac{d}{dx}(3x^3) + \frac{d}{dx}(x)
\]

Calculate each term:

- \(\frac{d}{dx}(x^4) = 4x^3\)
- \(\frac{d}{dx}(3x^3) = 9x^2\)
- \(\frac{d}{dx}(x) = 1\)

So,

\[
F'(x) = 4x^3 - 9x^2 + 1
\]

### Step 2: Find the Second Derivative (\( F''(x) \))

Differentiate \( F'(x) \) with respect to \( x \):

\[
F''(x) = \frac{d}{dx}(4x^3) - \frac{d}{dx}(9x^2) + \frac{d}{dx}(1)
\]

Calculate each term:

- \(\frac{d}{dx}(4x^3) = 12x^2\)
- \(\frac{d}{dx}(9x^2) = 18x\)
- \(\frac{d}{dx}(1) = 0\)

So,

\[
F''(x) = 12x^2 - 18x
\]

### Final Answer

The second derivative of \( F(x) \) is:

\[
\boxed{12x^2 - 18x}
\]

I need to find the derivative of x raised to the power of x, which is written as d/dx(x^x). 

First, I recognize that both the base and the exponent are functions of x. This type of problem can be approached using logarithmic differentiation because it simplifies the process when dealing with variables in both the base and the exponent.

I'll start by taking the natural logarithm of both sides of the equation y = x^x. Applying ln to both sides gives me ln(y) = ln(x^x).

Using the properties of logarithms, I can rewrite ln(x^x) as x times ln(x), so now the equation is ln(y) = x * ln(x).

Next, I'll differentiate both sides with respect to x. On the left side, applying the chain rule means differentiating ln(y) with respect to y and then multiplying by dy/dx. The derivative of ln(y) with respect to y is 1/y, so this becomes (1/y) * dy/dx.

On the right side, I'll use the product rule since it's x multiplied by ln(x). The product rule states that the derivative of u*v is u'v + uv', where u = x and v = ln(x).

The derivative of u = x with respect to x is 1. The derivative of v = ln(x) with respect to x is 1/x.

Putting it all together, the derivative of the right side is (1)*ln(x) + x*(1/x), which simplifies to ln(x) + 1.

Now, I'll set the derivatives equal to each other: (1/y) * dy/dx = ln(x) + 1.

To solve for dy/dx, I'll multiply both sides by y. This gives me dy/dx = y*(ln(x) + 1).

But since y was originally defined as x^x, substituting back in yields dy/dx = x^x*(ln(x) + 1).

Therefore, the derivative of x^x with respect to x is x^x multiplied by (ln(x) plus one).

To find the derivative of \( \frac{d}{dx}(x^x) \), we can use logarithmic differentiation.

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Let \( y = x^x \).**
   
2. **Take the natural logarithm of both sides:**
   \[
   \ln(y) = \ln(x^x)
   \]
   
3. **Simplify using logarithm properties (\( \ln(a^b) = b \cdot \ln(a) \)):**
   \[
   \ln(y) = x \cdot \ln(x)
   \]
   
4. **Differentiate both sides with respect to \( x \):**
   - Left side: Apply the chain rule.
     \[
     \frac{d}{dx}[\ln(y)] = \frac{1}{y} \cdot \frac{dy}{dx}
     \]
   - Right side: Apply the product rule (\( u = x \), \( v = \ln(x) \)).
     \[
     \frac{d}{dx}[x \cdot \ln(x)] = 1 \cdot \ln(x) + x \cdot \frac{1}{x} = \ln(x) + 1
     \]
   
5. **Set the derivatives equal to each other:**
   \[
   \frac{1}{y} \cdot \frac{dy}{dx} = \ln(x) + 1
   \]
   
6. **Solve for \( \frac{dy}{dx} \):**
   \[
   \frac{dy}{dx} = y \cdot (\ln(x) + 1)
   \]
   
7. **Substitute back \( y = x^x \):**
   \[
   \frac{dy}{dx} = x^x \cdot (\ln(x) + 1)
   \]

**Final Answer:**

The derivative of \( x^x \) with respect to \( x \) is:
\[
\boxed{x^{x}(\ln(x) + 1)}
\]

- The derivative of v with respect to x, v’(x), remains e^x since the derivative of e^x is itself.

Applying the product rule, the first derivative F’(x) becomes:
F’(x) = u’(x)v(x) + u(x)v’(x) = 2x e^x + x² e^x

Now, to find the second derivative F''(x), I need to differentiate F’(x). 

Looking at F’(x) = 2x e^x + x² e^x, it's again a sum of terms where each term is a product of functions. So, I'll apply the product rule to each part separately.

First Term: 2x e^x
- Let’s denote:
  - u₁(x) = 2x
  - v₁(x) = e^x
- Derivatives:
  - u₁’(x) = 2
  - v₁’(x) = e^x

Applying the product rule, the derivative of this term is:
d/dx [2x e^x] = 2 e^x + 2x e^x

Second Term: x² e^x
- Let’s denote:
  - u₂(x) = x²
  - v₂(x) = e^x
- Derivatives:
  - u₂’(x) = 2x
  - v₂’(x) = e^x

Applying the product rule, the derivative of this term is:
d/dx [x² e^x] = 2x e^x + x² e^x

Now, combining both derivatives from each term gives us F''(x):
F''(x) = (2 e^x + 2x e^x) + (2x e^x + x² e^x)

Simplifying by combining like terms:
F''(x) = 2 e^x + 4x e^x + x² e^x

Therefore, the second derivative of F(x) is e^x times (x squared plus four x plus two).
</think>

To find the second derivative of \( F(x) = x^2 e^x \), we'll use the product rule twice.

1. **First Derivative**:
   - Let \( u_1(x) = x^2 \) and \( v_1(x) = e^x \).
   - Apply the product rule: 
     \[
     F'(x) = u_1'(x)v_1(x) + u_1(x)v_1'(x) = 2x e^x + x^2 e^x
     \]

2. **Second Derivative**:
   - Differentiate \( F'(x) \):
     \[
     F''(x) = \frac{d}{dx}(2x e^x) + \frac{d}{dx}(x^2 e^x)
     \]
   - Apply the product rule to each term:
     - For \( 2x e^x \):
       \[
       \frac{d}{dx}(2x e^x) = 2e^x + 2x e^x
       \]
     - For \( x^2 e^x \):
       \[
       \frac{d}{dx}(x^2 e^x) = 2x e^x + x^2 e^x
       \]
   - Combine the results:
     \[
     F''(x) = (2e^x + 2x e^x) + (2x e^x + x^2 e^x) = 2e^x + 4x e^x + x^2 e^x
     \]
   - Factor out \( e^x \):
     \[
     F''(x) = e^x (x^2 + 4x + 2)
     \]

**Final Answer**:  
{'Final Answer': 'e^x (x² + 4x + 2)'}

   - Inner function: \( x^2 + 1 \)

\]

To find the derivative of the function \( F(x) = x^3 \ln x \), we'll use the **product rule** for differentiation. The product rule states that if you have a function \( h(x) = f(x) \cdot g(x) \), then the derivative \( h'(x) \) is given by:

\[
h'(x) = f'(x) \cdot g(x) + f(x) \cdot g'(x)
\]

**Step-by-Step Solution:**

1. **Identify the Functions:**
   - Let \( f(x) = x^3 \)
   - Let \( g(x) = \ln x \)

2. **Find the Derivatives of Each Function:**
   - The derivative of \( f(x) = x^3 \) with respect to \( x \) is:
     \[
     f'(x) = 3x^2
     \]
   - The derivative of \( g(x) = \ln x \) with respect to \( x \) is:
     \[
     g'(x) = \frac{1}{x}
     \]

3. **Apply the Product Rule:**
   \[
   F'(x) = f'(x) \cdot g(x) + f(x) \cdot g'(x)
   \]
   Substituting the derivatives we found:
   \[
   F'(x) = 3x^2 \cdot \ln x + x^3 \cdot \frac{1}{x}
   \]

4. **Simplify Each Term:**
   - \( 3x^2 \cdot \ln x \) remains as is.
   - \( x^3 \cdot \frac{1}{x} = x^{3-1} = x^2 \)

5. **Combine the Simplified Terms:**
   \[
   F'(x) = 3x^2 \ln x + x^2
   \]

6. **Factor Out Common Terms (Optional):**
   \[
   F'(x) = x^2 (3 \ln x + 1)
   \]

**Final Answer:**

\boxed{x^2(3 \ln x + 1)}

To find the derivative of \( F(x) = \frac{e^x}{1 + e^x} \), we apply the quotient rule. 

The quotient rule states that if \( F(x) = \frac{f(x)}{g(x)} \), then 
\[ F'(x) = \frac{f'(x)g(x) - f(x)g'(x)}{[g(x)]^2} \]

Here, \( f(x) = e^x \) and \( g(x) = 1 + e^x \). Their derivatives are:
- \( f'(x) = e^x \)
- \( g'(x) = e^x \)

Applying the quotient rule:
\[ F'(x) = \frac{e^x (1 + e^x) - e^x (e^x)}{(1 + e^x)^2} \]
Simplifying the numerator:
\[ e^x + e^{2x} - e^{2x} = e^x \]

Thus, the derivative is:
\[ F'(x) = \frac{e^x}{(1 + e^x)^2} \]

\[

\]

Solving for \( \frac{dy}{dx} \):
\[
\frac{dy}{dx} = \frac{1}{\sec^2(y)}
\]

Since \( \sec^2(y) = 1 + \tan^2(y) \) and \( \tan(y) = x \), we have:
\[
\sec^2(y) = 1 + x^2
\]

Therefore,
\[

\]

To find the derivative of the function \( f(x) = \frac{\sin x}{x^2 + 1} \), we will use the **quotient rule**. The quotient rule states that if you have a function \( \frac{u(x)}{v(x)} \), its derivative is given by:

\[
f'(x) = \frac{u'(x)v(x) - u(x)v'(x)}{[v(x)]^2}
\]

Applying this to our function:

1. **Identify** \( u(x) \) and \( v(x) \):
   - \( u(x) = \sin x \)
   - \( v(x) = x^2 + 1 \)

2. **Compute the derivatives**:
   - \( u'(x) = \cos x \)
   - \( v'(x) = 2x \)

3. **Apply the quotient rule formula**:
   \[
   f'(x) = \frac{\cos x (x^2 + 1) - \sin x (2x)}{(x^2 + 1)^2}
   \]

4. **Simplify if possible** (in this case, no further simplification is needed).

So, the derivative of \( f(x) = \frac{\sin x}{x^2 + 1} \) is:

\[
f'(x) = \frac{(x^2 + 1)\cos x - 2x \sin x}{(x^2 + 1)^2}
\]

To find the second derivative of \( F(x) = e^{x^2} \), we'll follow these steps:

### Step 1: Compute the First Derivative
We start by finding the first derivative \( F'(x) \).

\[
F(x) = e^{x^2}
\]

Using the chain rule:
\[
F'(x) = e^{x^2} \cdot \frac{d}{dx}(x^2) = 2x e^{x^2}
\]

### Step 2: Compute the Second Derivative
Next, we find the second derivative \( F''(x) \) by differentiating \( F'(x) \).

\[
F'(x) = 2x e^{x^2}
\]

We'll use the product rule to differentiate \( 2x e^{x^2} \). Let:
- \( u = 2x \)
- \( v = e^{x^2} \)

First, find the derivatives of \( u \) and \( v \):
\[
u' = 2
\]
\[
v' = e^{x^2} \cdot \frac{d}{dx}(x^2) = 2x e^{x^2}
\]

Now, apply the product rule:
\[
F''(x) = u'v + uv' = 2 \cdot e^{x^2} + (2x)(2x e^{x^2})
\]
\[
F''(x) = 2 e^{x^2} + 4x^2 e^{x^2}
\]

Factor out \( 2 e^{x^2} \):
\[
F''(x) = 2 e^{x^2}(1 + 2x^2)
\]
\[
F''(x) = 2e^{x^2}(1 + x^2)
\]

### Final Answer
The second derivative of \( F(x) = e^{x^2} \) is:

\[
\boxed{2e^{x^2}(1 + x^2)}
\]

First, identify the numerator (u) and denominator (v):
\( u = x \)
\( v = 1 + x^2 \)

Next, find their derivatives:
\( u' = 1 \)
\( v' = 2x \)

Apply the quotient rule formula:
\[
F'(x) = \frac{u'v - uv'}{v^2} = \frac{(1)(1 + x^2) - (x)(2x)}{(1 + x^2)^2}
\]

Simplify the numerator:
\( 1(1 + x^2) = 1 + x^2 \)
\( x(2x) = 2x^2 \)

Subtracting these gives:
\( 1 + x^2 - 2x^2 = 1 - x^2 \)

So, the derivative simplifies to:
\[
F'(x) = \frac{1 - x^2}{(1 + x^2)^2}
\]
</think>

To find the derivative of \( F(x) = \frac{x}{1 + x^2} \), we will use the **quotient rule** from calculus. The quotient rule states that if you have a function \( \frac{u(x)}{v(x)} \), its derivative is given by:

\[
F'(x) = \frac{u'(x)v(x) - u(x)v'(x)}{[v(x)]^2}
\]

**Step 1: Identify \( u(x) \) and \( v(x) \)**

Here, 
\[
u(x) = x
\]
and 
\[
v(x) = 1 + x^2
\]

**Step 2: Find the derivatives of \( u(x) \) and \( v(x) \)**

The derivative of \( u(x) = x \) is:
\[
u'(x) = 1
\]

The derivative of \( v(x) = 1 + x^2 \) is:
\[
v'(x) = 2x
\]

**Step 3: Apply the Quotient Rule**

Substitute \( u(x) \), \( u'(x) \), \( v(x) \), and \( v'(x) \) into the quotient rule formula:

\[
F'(x) = \frac{u'(x)v(x) - u(x)v'(x)}{[v(x)]^2} = \frac{(1)(1 + x^2) - (x)(2x)}{(1 + x^2)^2}
\]

**Step 4: Simplify the Numerator**

Calculate each term in the numerator:

\[
(1)(1 + x^2) = 1 + x^2
\]
\[
(x)(2x) = 2x^2
\]

Subtract these results:
\[
1 + x^2 - 2x^2 = 1 - x^2
\]

**Step 5: Write the Final Derivative**

Now, place the simplified numerator back into the denominator:

\[
F'(x) = \frac{1 - x^2}{(1 + x^2)^2}
\]

So, the derivative of \( F(x) = \frac{x}{1 + x^2} \) is:

**Final Answer:**  
\[
\boxed{\dfrac{1 - x^{2}}{(1 + x^{2})^{2}}}
\]

To find the limit of \(\sqrt{x^2 + 2x} - x\) as \(x\) approaches infinity:

\[
\lim_{{x \to \infty}} \left( \sqrt{x^2 + 2x} - x \right)
\]

**Step-by-Step Solution:**

1. **Factor out the highest power of \(x\) inside the square root:**
   
   \[
   \sqrt{x^2 + 2x} = x \cdot \sqrt{1 + \frac{2}{x}}
   \]
   
2. **Express the original expression using this factorization:**

   \[
   \sqrt{x^2 + 2x} - x = x \cdot \sqrt{1 + \frac{2}{x}} - x
   \]
   
3. **Factor out \(x\) from both terms:**
   
   \[
   x \left( \sqrt{1 + \frac{2}{x}} - 1 \right)
   \]
   
4. **Multiply and divide by the conjugate to rationalize:**

   \[
   \lim_{{x \to \infty}} \left[ \frac{x \cdot \left( \sqrt{1 + \frac{2}{x}} - 1 \right) \cdot \left( \sqrt{1 + \frac{2}{x}} + 1 \right)}{\sqrt{1 + \frac{2}{x}} + 1} \right]
   \]
   
5. **Simplify the numerator using the difference of squares:**

   \[
   x \cdot \left( 1 + \frac{2}{x} - 1 \right) = x \cdot \frac{2}{x} = 2
   \]
   
6. **The expression now becomes:**
   
   \[
   \lim_{{x \to \infty}} \left( \frac{2}{\sqrt{1 + \frac{2}{x}} + 1} \right)
   \]
   
7. **Evaluate the limit as \(x\) approaches infinity:**

   As \(x \to \infty\), \(\frac{2}{x} \to 0\), so:
   
   \[
   \sqrt{1 + 0} = 1
   \]
   
8. **Substitute this back into the expression:**
   
   \[
   \frac{2}{1 + 1} = 1
   \]
   
**Final Answer:**

\[
\boxed{1}
\]

Step-by-Step Explanation:

To evaluate the limit:
\[
\lim_{{x \to 0}} \frac{\ln(1 + x)}{x}
\]

1. **Use Taylor Series Expansion**:
   The natural logarithm can be expanded around \(x = 0\) as:
   \[
   \ln(1 + x) = x - \frac{x^2}{2} + \frac{x^3}{3} - \cdots
   \]
   
2. **Substitute the Expansion**:
   Substitute this into the limit expression:
   \[
   \lim_{{x \to 0}} \frac{x - \frac{x^2}{2} + \frac{x^3}{3} - \cdots}{x}
   \]
   
3. **Simplify the Expression**:
   Divide each term by \(x\):
   \[
   = \lim_{{x \to 0}} \left(1 - \frac{x}{2} + \frac{x^2}{3} - \cdots \right)
   \]
   
4. **Evaluate the Limit**:
   As \(x\) approaches 0, all terms involving \(x\) vanish, leaving:
   \[
   = 1
   \]

So, the limit is:
\[
\boxed{1}
\]

To determine the limit of \( x^x \) as \( x \) approaches 0 from the positive side, I start by recognizing that directly substituting \( x = 0 \) results in an indeterminate form \( 0^0 \). To resolve this, I'll rewrite the expression using the natural logarithm and exponential functions.

Taking the natural logarithm of both sides, we have:
\[ \ln(y) = \lim_{x \to 0^+} x \cdot \ln(x) \]

Next, I analyze the limit \( \lim_{x \to 0^+} x \cdot \ln(x) \). As \( x \) approaches 0 from the positive side, \( \ln(x) \) tends to negative infinity. However, multiplying this by \( x \), which is approaching zero, leads us to evaluate the behavior of their product.

To better understand this limit, I can consider substituting \( t = -\ln(x) \). As \( x \to 0^+ \), \( t \to +\infty \), and the limit becomes:
\[ \lim_{t \to +\infty} e^{-t} \cdot (-t) = 0 \]

Since this inner limit approaches zero, exponentiating it gives:
\[ y = e^{0} = 1 \]

Thus, the original limit \( \lim_{x \to 0^+} x^x \) is equal to 1.

I need to find the limit of 2^x divided by x^3 as x approaches infinity.

First, I'll analyze both the numerator and the denominator separately. As x grows larger without bound, 2^x increases exponentially because any number greater than 1 raised to an infinite power will tend towards infinity. On the other hand, x^3 is a polynomial function that also tends to infinity as x approaches infinity, but its growth rate is much slower compared to exponential functions.

Next, I'll consider the behavior of both parts when x becomes very large. The numerator 2^x will become extremely large, while the denominator x^3, although growing without bound, does so at a much slower pace than the numerator. This suggests that as x approaches infinity, the value of 2^x will dominate over x^3.

To better understand this relationship, I'll apply L’Hôpital’s Rule since both the numerator and denominator approach infinity, resulting in an indeterminate form of ∞/∞. L’Hôpital’s Rule allows us to differentiate the numerator and the denominator repeatedly until we can determine the limit.

Differentiating the numerator 2^x with respect to x gives ln(2) * 2^x, while differentiating the denominator x^3 three times results in a constant multiple of 6 (since d/dx x^3 = 3x^2, d²/dx² x^3 = 6x, and d³/dx³ x^3 = 6). After applying L’Hôpital’s Rule three times, the expression becomes [ln(2)^3 * 2^x] / 6.

As x approaches infinity, ln(2)^3 remains a constant multiplier, but 2^x still tends to infinity. Therefore, the limit of the entire expression is unbounded and tends towards infinity.
</think>

1. **Exponential Growth vs Polynomial Growth**:
   - The term \(2^x\) grows exponentially with respect to \(x\), which is significantly faster than any polynomial function like \(x^3\).

2. **Application of Limit Laws**:
   - As \(x\) approaches infinity, \(2^x\) will dominate over \(x^3\) because exponential functions outgrow polynomial functions.

3. **Conclusion**:
   - Therefore, the limit is unbounded and tends towards infinity.

\[
\lim_{{x \to 1}} \frac{\sqrt[3]{(1 + (x-1))^3} - 1}{x - 1} = \lim_{{h \to 0}} \frac{(1 + h) - 1}{h} = \lim_{{h \to 0}} \frac{h}{h} = 1
\]

Thus, the limit is 1.

To find the derivative of \( F(x) = 3x^2 - 5x + 4 \), we will use basic differentiation rules.

1. **Differentiate each term separately**:
   
   - The derivative of \( 3x^2 \):
     \[
     \frac{d}{dx}(3x^2) = 6x
     \]
     
   - The derivative of \( -5x \):
     \[
     \frac{d}{dx}(-5x) = -5
     \]
     
   - The derivative of the constant term \( 4 \):
     \[
     \frac{d}{dx}(4) = 0
     \]

2. **Combine the derivatives**:
   
   \[
   F'(x) = 6x - 5 + 0 = 6x - 5
   \]

So, the derivative of \( F(x) = 3x^2 - 5x + 4 \) is:

\[
F'(x) = 6x - 5
\]

To find the derivative of \( \frac{1}{x} \) with respect to \( x \), I'll start by rewriting the expression using negative exponents, so it becomes \( x^{-1} \).

Next, I'll apply the power rule for differentiation. The power rule states that if you have a function in the form \( f(x) = x^n \), its derivative is \( f'(x) = n \cdot x^{n-1} \).

Using this rule on \( x^{-1} \), where \( n = -1 \), the derivative will be \( -1 \cdot x^{-2} \), which simplifies to \( -\frac{1}{x^2} \).
</think>

To find the derivative of \( \frac{1}{x} \) with respect to \( x \), we can use basic differentiation rules.

**Step-by-Step Solution:**

1. **Express the Function in Exponential Form:**
   
   We start by rewriting \( \frac{1}{x} \) using a negative exponent.
   \[
   f(x) = \frac{1}{x} = x^{-1}
   \]

2. **Apply the Power Rule:**
   
   The power rule states that if \( f(x) = x^n \), then the derivative \( f'(x) \) is:
   \[
   f'(x) = n \cdot x^{n-1}
   \]
   Here, \( n = -1 \).

3. **Differentiate:**
   
   Substituting \( n = -1 \) into the power rule formula:
   \[
   f'(x) = (-1) \cdot x^{-1 - 1} = -x^{-2}
   \]
   
4. **Simplify the Expression:**
   
   Rewrite the expression in terms of fractions.
   \[
   f'(x) = -\frac{1}{x^2}
   \]

**Final Answer:**

The derivative of \( \frac{1}{x} \) with respect to \( x \) is:
\[
\boxed{-\dfrac{1}{x^{2}}}
\]

f'(x) = lim(h→0) [f(x + h) - f(x)] / h

So if f(x) = sin(x), then f'(x) would be the limit as h approaches 0 of [sin(x + h) - sin(x)] divided by h.

I can use a trigonometric identity to simplify the numerator. The identity is:

sin(A + B) = sin(A)cos(B) + cos(A)sin(B)

Applying this, sin(x + h) becomes sin(x)cos(h) + cos(x)sin(h).

f'(x) = lim(h→0) [sin(x)cos(h) + cos(x)sin(h) - sin(x)] / h

Simplify the numerator by combining like terms:

= lim(h→0) [sin(x)(cos(h) - 1) + cos(x)sin(h)] / h

Now, split this into two separate limits:

= lim(h→0) [sin(x)(cos(h) - 1)/h] + lim(h→0) [cos(x)sin(h)/h]

I can factor out sin(x) from the first limit and cos(x) from the second.

Now, I remember that as h approaches 0:

- lim(h→0) (cos(h) - 1)/h = 0
- lim(h→0) sin(h)/h = 1

Therefore, substituting these back into the expression:

= sin(x) * 0 + cos(x) * 1

Which simplifies to:

f'(x) = cos(x)

To find the derivative of the function \( f(x) = \frac{x^4}{4} - 6x^3 - 5x^2 + x + 17 \), we will apply the power rule to each term individually. The power rule states that if \( f(x) = x^n \), then \( f'(x) = n \cdot x^{n-1} \).

Let's differentiate each term of the function:

\[
\begin{align*}
\frac{d}{dx}\left(\frac{x^4}{4}\right) &= \frac{4x^3}{4} = x^3 \\
\frac{d}{dx}(-6x^3) &= -18x^2 \\
\frac{d}{dx}(-5x^2) &= -10x \\
\frac{d}{dx}(x) &= 1 \\
\frac{d}{dx}(17) &= 0
\end{align*}
\]

Now, combine these derivatives to get the final result:

\[
f'(x) = x^3 - 18x^2 - 10x + 1
\]

First, I need to find the second derivative of the function \( F(x) = x^4 - 3x^3 + x \).

To do this, I'll start by finding the first derivative. Using the power rule for differentiation:

The derivative of \( x^n \) is \( n \cdot x^{n-1} \). Applying this to each term:

- The derivative of \( x^4 \) is \( 4x^3 \).
- The derivative of \( -3x^3 \) is \( -9x^2 \).
- The derivative of \( x \) is \( 1 \).

So, the first derivative \( F'(x) \) is:
\[ F'(x) = 4x^3 - 9x^2 + 1 \]

Next, I'll find the second derivative by differentiating \( F'(x) \):

- The derivative of \( 4x^3 \) is \( 12x^2 \).
- The derivative of \( -9x^2 \) is \( -18x \).

To find the **second derivative** of the function:

\[
F(x) = x^4 - 3x^3 + x
\]

we follow these steps:

### Step 1: Find the First Derivative (\( F'(x) \))

Using the power rule for differentiation, which states that \( \frac{d}{dx} x^n = n \cdot x^{n-1} \), we differentiate each term of \( F(x) \):

\[
F'(x) = \frac{d}{dx}(x^4) - 3 \cdot \frac{d}{dx}(x^3) + \frac{d}{dx}(x)
\]

Calculating each derivative:

- \( \frac{d}{dx}(x^4) = 4x^3 \)
- \( \frac{d}{dx}(x^3) = 3x^2 \), so \( -3 \cdot 3x^2 = -9x^2 \)
- \( \frac{d}{dx}(x) = 1 \)

Putting it all together:

\[
F'(x) = 4x^3 - 9x^2 + 1
\]

### Step 2: Find the Second Derivative (\( F''(x) \))

Now, we differentiate \( F'(x) \):

\[
F''(x) = \frac{d}{dx}(4x^3) - \frac{d}{dx}(9x^2) + \frac{d}{dx}(1)
\]

Calculating each derivative:

- \( \frac{d}{dx}(4x^3) = 12x^2 \)
- \( \frac{d}{dx}(9x^2) = 18x \), so \( -9x^2 \) becomes \( -18x \)
- The derivative of a constant (1) is 0.

Putting it all together:

\[
F''(x) = 12x^2 - 18x
\]

### Final Answer

\[
\boxed{12x^2 - 18x}
\]

To find the derivative of \( x^x \), we can use logarithmic differentiation. Here's a step-by-step solution:

**Step 1: Rewrite the Function Using Exponentials and Natural Logarithms**

First, recognize that:
\[ x^x = e^{x \ln(x)} \]

This allows us to differentiate more easily.

**Step 2: Differentiate Using the Chain Rule**

Let \( u = x \ln(x) \). Then:
\[ \frac{d}{dx} (e^u) = e^u \cdot \frac{du}{dx} \]

So, we need to find \( \frac{du}{dx} \).

**Step 3: Differentiate \( u = x \ln(x) \) Using the Product Rule**

The product rule states that:
\[ \frac{d}{dx}[f g] = f' g + f g' \]

Here, let:
- \( f = x \)
- \( g = \ln(x) \)

Then:
- \( f' = 1 \)
- \( g' = \frac{1}{x} \)

Applying the product rule:
\[ \frac{du}{dx} = (1)(\ln(x)) + (x)\left(\frac{1}{x}\right) = \ln(x) + 1 \]

**Step 4: Combine the Results**

Substitute \( u = x \ln(x) \) and \( \frac{du}{dx} = \ln(x) + 1 \) back into the derivative:
\[ \frac{d}{dx}(e^u) = e^{x \ln(x)} (\ln(x) + 1) \]

Since \( e^{x \ln(x)} = x^x \), we have:
\[ \frac{d}{dx}(x^x) = x^x (\ln(x) + 1) \]

**Final Answer**

\[

\]

\[

\]

\[
u' = 2x

\[

v_1' = e^x

\[
F''(x) = (2 + 2x)e^x + (2x + x^2)e^x

\[
h'(x) = f'(g(x)) \cdot g'(x)
\]

**Step-by-Step Solution:**

1. **Identify the Outer and Inner Functions:**
   
   - Let \( f(u) = e^u \), where \( u = g(x) = x^2 \).

2. **Differentiate the Outer Function:**
   
   \[
   f'(u) = \frac{d}{du} e^u = e^u
   \]

3. **Differentiate the Inner Function:**
   
   \[
   g'(x) = \frac{d}{dx} x^2 = 2x
   \]

4. **Apply the Chain Rule:**
   
   Multiply the derivative of the outer function evaluated at \( u = x^2 \) by the derivative of the inner function:
   
   \[
   h'(x) = f'(g(x)) \cdot g'(x) = e^{x^2} \cdot 2x
   \]

**Final Answer:**

To find the derivative of \( F(x) = \frac{\ln x}{x^2} \), we will use the **quotient rule**. The quotient rule states that if you have a function \( \frac{f(x)}{g(x)} \), its derivative is:

\[
\left( \frac{f}{g} \right)' = \frac{f'(x)g(x) - f(x)g'(x)}{[g(x)]^2}
\]

**Step 1: Identify \( f(x) \) and \( g(x) \)**

- Numerator (\( f(x) \)): \( \ln x \)
- Denominator (\( g(x) \)): \( x^2 \)

**Step 2: Compute the derivatives of \( f(x) \) and \( g(x) \)**

- \( f'(x) = \frac{1}{x} \)
- \( g'(x) = 2x \)

**Step 3: Apply the Quotient Rule**

\[
F'(x) = \frac{\left( \frac{1}{x} \right)(x^2) - (\ln x)(2x)}{(x^2)^2}
\]

Simplify each term:

- \( \left( \frac{1}{x} \right)(x^2) = x \)
- \( (\ln x)(2x) = 2x \ln x \)

So,

\[
F'(x) = \frac{x - 2x \ln x}{x^4}
\]

Factor out an \( x \) from the numerator:

\[
F'(x) = \frac{x(1 - 2 \ln x)}{x^4} = \frac{1 - 2 \ln x}{x^3}
\]

**Final Answer:**

To find the derivative of F(x) = (e^x)/(1 + e^x), I'll apply the quotient rule.

- dv/dx = e^x

To find the derivative of \( F(x) = \frac{e^x}{1 + e^x} \), we'll use the **quotient rule**. 

### Step-by-Step Solution:

1. **Identify the numerator and denominator:**
   - Numerator (\( u \)) = \( e^x \)
   - Denominator (\( v \)) = \( 1 + e^x \)

2. **Find the derivatives of the numerator and denominator:**
   - \( \frac{du}{dx} = e^x \)
   - \( \frac{dv}{dx} = e^x \) (since the derivative of \( 1 \) is 0)

3. **Apply the quotient rule formula:**
   
   \[
   F'(x) = \frac{v \cdot \frac{du}{dx} - u \cdot \frac{dv}{dx}}{v^2}
   \]
   
4. **Substitute the values into the formula:**
   
   \[
   F'(x) = \frac{(1 + e^x)(e^x) - e^x(e^x)}{(1 + e^x)^2}
   \]

5. **Simplify the numerator:**
   
   \[
   (1 + e^x)e^x - e^{2x} = e^x + e^{2x} - e^{2x} = e^x
   \]

6. **Final simplified form of the derivative:**
   
   \[
   F'(x) = \frac{e^x}{(1 + e^x)^2}
   \]

### Final Answer:
\boxed{\dfrac{e^x}{\left(1+e^x\right)^2}}

Hmm, but I still have cos²(y) in terms of y. Since tan(y) = x, maybe I can express cos(y) in terms of x. Let's think about a right triangle where angle y has opposite side length x and adjacent side length 1 because tan(y) = opposite/adjacent = x/1.

In this triangle, the hypotenuse would be sqrt(x² + 1). So cos(y) is adjacent over hypotenuse, which is 1/sqrt(x² + 1). Therefore, cos²(y) is (1/sqrt(x² + 1)) squared, which is 1/(x² + 1).

So substituting back into dy/dx, I get 1 divided by (1/(x² + 1)), which simplifies to x² + 1.

Wait, but that seems like the denominator. Let me check my steps again because earlier when solving for dy/dx, it was 1 / sec²(y), and since sec²(y) is 1 + tan²(y) by the Pythagorean identity. Since tan(y) = x, then sec²(y) = 1 + x². Therefore, 1/sec²(y) would be 1/(1 + x²).

Ah, so that makes dy/dx equal to 1 over (1 + x²). That seems more consistent with what I remember from calculus.

So putting it all together: the derivative of arctan(x) is 1 divided by (1 plus x squared). So in LaTeX, that's \frac{1}{1 + x^2}.

The derivative of \(\tan^{-1} x\) with respect to \(x\) can be found using implicit differentiation. By setting \(y = \tan^{-1} x\), we have \(\tan y = x\). Differentiating both sides and applying the chain rule gives us \(\sec^2 y \cdot \frac{dy}{dx} = 1\). Solving for \(\frac{dy}{dx}\) yields \(\frac{1}{\sec^2 y}\), which simplifies to \(\frac{1}{1 + x^2}\).

The derivative of F(x) = x ln x is found using the product rule. 

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Identify f(x) and g(x):**
   - f(x) = x
   - g(x) = ln x

2. **Compute the derivatives:**
   - f'(x) = 1 (derivative of x)
   - g'(x) = 1/x (derivative of ln x)

3. **Apply the product rule:**
   F'(x) = f'(x) * g(x) + f(x) * g'(x)
          = 1 * ln x + x * (1/x)
          = ln x + 1

**Final Answer:**  
{'Final Answer': 'ln(x) + 1'}

To find the second derivative of \( F(x) = e^{x^2} \), we'll proceed step by step.

**First Derivative:**

Using the chain rule:
\[
F'(x) = \frac{d}{dx} e^{x^2} = e^{x^2} \cdot \frac{d}{dx}(x^2) = 2x e^{x^2}
\]

**Second Derivative:**

Now, differentiate \( F'(x) = 2x e^{x^2} \) using the product rule:
\[
F''(x) = \frac{d}{dx} (2x) \cdot e^{x^2} + 2x \cdot \frac{d}{dx}(e^{x^2})
\]
\[
= 2 \cdot e^{x^2} + 2x \cdot (2x e^{x^2}) = 2e^{x^2} + 4x^2 e^{x^2}
\]

Factor out \( 2e^{x^2} \):
\[
F''(x) = 2e^{x^2}(1 + 2x^2)
\]

To find the derivative of \( F(x) = \frac{x}{1 + x^2} \), we apply the quotient rule. 

The quotient rule states that if \( F(x) = \frac{u}{v} \), then:
\[
F'(x) = \frac{v \cdot u' - u \cdot v'}{v^2}
\]

Here, let:
- \( u = x \) ⇒ \( u' = 1 \)
- \( v = 1 + x^2 \) ⇒ \( v' = 2x \)

Applying the quotient rule:
\[
F'(x) = \frac{(1 + x^2)(1) - x(2x)}{(1 + x^2)^2} = \frac{1 + x^2 - 2x^2}{(1 + x^2)^2} = \frac{1 - x^2}{(1 + x^2)^2}
\]

So, the derivative is:
\[
F'(x) = \frac{1 - x^2}{(1 + x^2)^2}
\]

To find the derivative of sec(x), I recall that sec(x) is the reciprocal of cos(x). 

Using the chain rule, I can differentiate this. The outer function is 1/u and its derivative is -1/u² times the derivative of the inner function u.

The derivative of cos(x) with respect to x is -sin(x).

To find the derivative of \(\sec x\), we can use the chain rule. Here's a step-by-step explanation:

**Step 1:** Recall that \(\sec x = \frac{1}{\cos x}\).

**Step 2:** Differentiate using the chain rule.

Let \(u = \cos x\), so \(\sec x = \frac{1}{u}\).

The derivative of \(\frac{1}{u}\) with respect to \(u\) is \(-\frac{1}{u^2}\).

**Step 3:** Multiply by the derivative of \(u\) with respect to \(x\):

\[
\frac{d}{dx} (\sec x) = -\frac{1}{u^2} \cdot (-\sin x) = \frac{\sin x}{\cos^2 x}
\]

**Step 4:** Simplify the expression:

\[
\frac{\sin x}{\cos^2 x} = \sec x \tan x
\]

**Final Answer:**

\boxed{\sec(x)\tan(x)}
